RAJYA SABHA 
Tuesday, the 29th July, 2025/7 Sravana , 1947 (Saka) 
The House met at eleven of the clock, 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN in the Chair. 
FELICITATION BY THE CHAIR 
MR. DEPUTY CHAIRMAN: Hon.  Members, it is  a matter of immense joy for all of us 
that Sushri Divya Deshmukh has made the nation  proud by winning the prestigious 
Chess World Cup. At a young age, she has displayed remarkable skill, resilience and 
strategic brilliance. Her victory is not just a personal milestone but a defining moment 
for Indian chess, reflecting the growing strength of women in arenas once dominated 
by men. Her journey is an inspiration to young aspirants across the country, 
encouraging them to dream big and persever e. On behalf of the entire House, I 
extend heartfelt congratulations to Sushri Divya Deshmukh, her coaches, family and 
the Indian chess fraternity for this remarkable achievement.

(ii)    A copy each (in English and Hindi) of the following Notifications of the Ministry

that terror, trade and talks cannot go together; water and blood cannot flow together.

Minister, Shri Jagan Mohan Reddy Garu, expressed his deep  anguish and said, 
“Shocked to hear about the terror attack in  Pahalgam, condemn this cowardly act of 
violence. My thoughts are with the victim s and their families. Praying for the speedy 
recovery of those injured. ” Among the victims, two of th em were residents of Andhra 
Pradesh – one Mr. Chandramouli, a retire d bank employee and the other one, Mr. 
Madhusudhan from Kavali, Nellore District. Their families, like countless others, grieve 
but also stand proud today because India did not remain silent.

Operation Sindoor, per se. India launched this operation in retaliation to the Pahalgam 
terrorist attack in April 2025. The name fo r this operation was also chosen as a 
personal tribute to the victims, particularly , the widows of Pahalgam attack. This has 
soothed feelings of the families of those wh o were killed in the Pahalgam attack. The 
operation targeted nine terrorist infrastr ucture sites in Pakistan and Pakistan-
occupied Jammu and Kashmir.  The Operation Sindoor was a coordinated, precision 
strike operation launched by the Indian Armed Forces on 7 th May, 2025.  At last, the 
final operation was executed in 22 minutes.  It highlighted India's technological 
capabilities. Moreover, I am very happy to note that no Indian assets were lost during 
the strikes. This was made clear by the Nati onal Security Advisor’s talk at Madras IIT 
a week ago. He asked the critics, “Show even a small broken  glass on the Indian 
side.” Nothing happened. Sir, Operation Sindoor  sent a clear message to Pakistan  
that no further acts of terrorism will be  taken lightly and such acts will not be 
considered as proxies. The Indian Armed Forces targeted terrorist facilities and 
leaders linked with Jaish-e-Mohammed, Lashkar-e-Taiba and Hizbul Mujahideen in 
Pakistan and Pakistan-occupied Jammu and Kashmir. The strike aimed to dismantle 
the terrorist infrastructure used for planning attacks against India.  It did not target any 
civilian areas nor did it harm any civilians . I deeply appreciate hon. Prime Minister, 
Shri Narendra Modi, for keeping the operat ional ethics which were central to the 
mission and restraint was exercised to avoid harm to civilians. There is no doubt that 
India became victorious in this operation like earlier surgical strikes. India's offensive 
strikes were such that the runways of those bases had been damaged in Pakistan and 
even now they could not be used after th e expiry of more than  two months of the 
operation. Sir, the indigenously-develop ed Akash surface-to-air missile system 
proved very effective. In total, Indi a's response was deliberate, precise, and 
strategic, without crossing the Line of Control or international boundary, Indian forces 
struck terrorists’ infrastructure and eliminat ed multiple terrorists. Operation Sindoor is 
not just a story of tactical success; it is  a validation of India's defence indigenous 
policies.

SHRI P. CHIDAMBARAM (Tamil Nadu): Sir, I r ealise that I am following the Leader of

[THE VICE-CHAIRMAN (SHRI P. WILSON) in the Chair.] 
 
Sir, I am glad that we are eventually discussing Operation Sindoor. Ever since 
Operation Sindoor ended, we have been  demanding that a special session of 
Parliament be called to di scuss Operation Sindoor. But the Government, for reasons 
best known to it, refused to call a special session. All over the world, in every 
democratic country, where a war has taken pl ace, even during the war, Parliament is 
in session. Example is the United Kingdo m. And, certainly, after the war, the 
Parliament is convened to discuss various aspects of the war.   
If the subject is ‘India's Strong, Succe ssful and Decisive Operation Sindoor in 
response to a terrorist attack in Pahalgam, there can be no quarrel with that subject. 
The subject is really the aftermath of Operation Sindoor, the consequences of 
Operation Sindoor. On the subject itself, if you ask me, ‘Was Operation Sindoor 
strong?’, I would say, ‘Yes’.  Was Operat ion Sindoor successful? I would say, ‘Yes’. 
If you ask me, ‘Was Operation Sindoor decisi ve?’, I can only say, only time will tell. 
Whether Operation Sindoor was decisive, only time will tell.   
I am reminded of a decisive war. On December 16, 1971, there was a historic 
victory. General Niazi surrendered to Gene ral Jagjit Singh Arora, along with 93,000 
soldiers.  It was the largest surrender of Pr isoners of War after the World War II. That 
was a decisive victory. On the contrary, Operation Sindoor ended with a ceasefire!  
122
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

The India-Pakistan war over Bangladesh ended with a surrender of the Pakistani 
forces in favour of India. Operation Sindoor  ended with a ceasefire!  I think the people 
of India know the difference between ‘su rrender’ and ‘ceasefire’.  The Government 
claimed, and it may be accepted, that India has destroyed terrorist infrastructure in 
Pakistan in nine places, -- we accepted -- and that India severely damaged 11 
military airbases -- we accepted.  But, why did India agree to a ceasefire? That is the 
question. The consequences of Operation Sindoor and the aftermath of Operation 
Sindoor is a question.  Please remember  that the Congress Working Committee was 
called after the Pahalgam attack and we ex tended unconditional and full support to 
the Government; so did the INDIA bloc parties. Nobody had any reservation in 
entrusting to the Government full author ity, full power and full support to take 
whatever action it chose fit. I wrote tw o columns on May 4 and May 11. In fact, a 
couple of my friends commented that the columns were very soft on Government.  I 
said, it may be but that was consistent with my party line.  This was consistent with 
my belief that in a war like situation, we must trust the Government, we must repose 
faith in the Government and allow the Government to take whatever action it needs to 
take.   
Sir, I must record that the military lead ership of India was exemplary. Let me 
repeat that the military le adership of India was exemplary and not only during 
Operation Sindoor but also after Operation Sindoor, the military leadership has been 
candid and forthright. The Chief of Defence Staff admitted tactical mistakes. He also 
said that after the tactical mistakes, they paused, the military leaders got together and 
re-strategized. He admitted losses which is why I call the CDS and other military 
leaders and their leadership exemplary. I call them candid, forthright and transparent 
because he admitted tactical mistakes, re-strategized and admitted losses. The 
Deputy Chief of Army Staff admitted that Chinese aircraft had been deployed. He also 
admitted that Chinese missiles had been de ployed. He also admitted that drones, 
and we gather these were from Turkey, were  deployed.  If true, and I see no reason 
to disbelieve the Chief of Defence Staff and the Deputy Chief of Army Staff, if Chinese 
aircraft J-10 and Chinese missiles PL-15 were deployed by Pakistan, the only 
conclusion is, China was testing its military hardware in the India-Pakistan conflict.  
The Congress Working Committee which met later pointed out that there was an 
intelligence and security failure. But the Government has so far not acknowledged the 
security failure and the intelligence failure . Please remember -- I think, Milind and 
Praful mentioned the Mumbai terror attack -- I took over as Home Minister the day 
after the Mumbai terror attack was ended on December 1.  Within a few days, I flew to 
Mumbai and, I think, Milind was with me.  I flew to Mumbai and in the first press

conference, I said: “I admit security failure, intelligence failure and I regret the failure.”

132
[ RAJYA SABHA ]

would have responded in measure. Having said  that, I think there is a lot of learning 
that the Government and all of us would need to take from it. First, we need to accept

cent of our GDP to defence. As some of the Members and hon. Chidambaram ji 
spoke, there is a fused front. We have Pakistan and China. If China is not joining the 
war, it is equipping Pakistan. Sir, we need  to understand that we are facing a greater 
threat. We are through the peace times. The world is moving into this world of 
uncertainty. India must brace itself.   
Sir, Europe today has decided that, in principle, it will increase its defence 
GDP to three per cent. Today India is spendi ng 1.9 per cent. I think as a Parliament 
and as Members of Rajya Sabha, we all need to support and move towards India 
eventually spending three per cent or even more, but it ha s to be done incrementally. 
Out of the 1.9 per cent that we spend on defence, only 26 per cent goes on capital 
investment and acquisition. There has to be an increase. My second suggestion is 
this: We are now seeing a changed paradi gm where countries are engaged in full-
spectrum warfare. There is space and tec hnology warfare; there is information 
warfare; there is cyber warfare. I just want ed to give some numbers for the House to 
have a sense.  India has 20,000 certified cyber professionals; China has one lakh 
cyber professionals. India needs to train one lakh cyber professionals by 2030.  
  Sir, the Director of Research and Operations, Centre for Research on Cyber 
Intelligence and Digital Forensics say that if China is spending 15 billion dollars of its 
annual budget on cyber-related expenditure, and if India’s GDP is one-fifth the size of 
China's GDP, it translates to 3 billion dolla rs, which is Rs. 24,000 crores. Our current 
budget is only Rs. 739 crores allocated fo r cyber security. We need to increase the 
cyber security allocations. We need to invest in drones, in AI and we need to enhance 
the budgetary spend in this critical area.   
The last of the lessons that I would ask the House to consider is the limitation 
of diplomatic capital. We realise that in time s of crisis, like this one, India must make 
strategic choices, just as we have done du ring previous wars, on whether to support 
or be part of a conflict. Now, as they say,  the proof of the pudding is in the eating. 
When we confronted a crisis -- perhaps, there were some sympathetic ears -- we 
did not receive any overt, extended suppor t. Nobody condemned Pakistan directly.  
What India needs to learn is this: The bigger our economic heft, the better we 
progress economically, the more seriously th e world will take us. We will not need 
anybody; we will defend ourselves. Let us spend more on defence. Let us work 
towards improving our economy.
